November 2026

Thursday, November 12, 2026

Just the Tip: The FCC, the Carriers, and the End of the Toll Booth

On October 29, the FCC voted 3–0 to propose letting satellite operators reach ordinary phones without renting spectrum from the phone companies, and the phone companies had already filed, a week before the vote, to strip the two biggest bands out of the proposal. Nothing was granted. It is only a proposal, only a comment period, only 482 megahertz of eligibility. Just the tip. Everybody in the docket knows what the tip is for. The 2024 rules made AT&T, Verizon, and T-Mobile the landlords of every satellite conversation with a normal phone, and the new proposal retires the landlords. The carriers will survive the satellite business. The rent is what dies, and the rent is the part they filed to protect.

The source video is Joseph Cristina at DISHYtech, Starlink Mobile Just Won! The Big 3 Are Done. His headline runs ahead of the record, and the record, checked against the FCC's own documents, is more interesting than the headline.

What the FCC actually did

The October 29 vote adopted a Notice of Proposed Rulemaking, FCC-26-71, unanimously. It proposes expanding the Supplemental Coverage from Space framework from five bands and 344 megahertz to thirteen bands and 826, adding 482 megahertz of eligibility, including the 2.5 GHz and 3.45 GHz mid-bands. It proposes harmonizing the satellite rules. And it proposes replacing the 2024 framework's central mechanism: today a satellite operator may work in a terrestrial band only by leasing spectrum from the terrestrial licensees who hold that band, all of them, across a whole geographic area. The proposal swaps the lease for a certification, the operator attesting that it has coexistence agreements or holds the licenses itself, in smaller service areas. Comments run into January. Final rules need a second vote, sometime in 2027. Commissioner Gomez voted yes and said on the record that she does not want the framework to let one or two companies dominate the market. She is tracking the right variable.

Three weeks earlier, on October 6, the FCC's bureaus had already granted SpaceX the working prototype of the new model: DA 26-1078, a license for 15,000 direct-to-cell satellites, with the lease requirement waived for the bands SpaceX bought outright from EchoStar for roughly $19.6 billion. The license is real and it is conditioned: the EchoStar bands stay test-only until that transaction finishes closing, expected around November 30, 2027, half the fleet is due by October 2032, all of it by October 2035, and not one of the 15,000 has launched. Authorization is the starting gun. The video sells it as the finish line, and it is the starting gun.

The petition, honestly

The carriers' move was a joint ex parte letter from their trade association, CTIA, filed October 22 after meetings with all three commissioners' offices on October 20. It asked the Commission to remove the 2.5 and 3.45 GHz bands, 290 of the 482 megahertz, from the proposal before the vote. T-Mobile filed separately on October 21 with the technical case: terrestrial 5G in those bands runs synchronized time-division, every tower transmitting and listening on one shared clock, and a satellite's signal arrives late and Doppler-shifted from a platform moving seven and a half kilometers a second, which can drop downlink energy into the slots where towers are listening. The interference mechanism is real engineering. T-Mobile's magnitudes, up to 96 percent of a cell's uplink coverage lost, are T-Mobile's modeling in an advocacy filing, and the FCC has adopted none of them. The Commission kept both bands in the proposal and sent the argument to the comment round. The carriers lost the ambush and kept the war.

Just the tip

There is an old joke about a negotiation conducted one inch at a time, and every regulated industry in America knows it by heart, because every regulated industry has run it. Just a proposal. Just a comment period. Just an eligibility list. The party being entered learns the truth at the pace the process allows, and the process is designed so that each individual step is too small to justify a war. The carriers have used this exact instrument for forty years, one waiver, one ruling, one leased band at a time. They recognized the shape of October 29 instantly, the way you recognize your own handwriting. That is why the filing came before the vote, why it aimed at 60 percent of the megahertz, and why it argued architecture instead of price. You do not mobilize three legal departments over a tip. You mobilize over what the tip is attached to.

The velodrome

Track sprint racing works like this. Two riders on a velodrome go super slow, super slow, super slow, sometimes stopping dead on the banking, because the rider in front does all the work and the rider behind rides free in the slipstream. The race moves at the speed of the man who refuses to lead. Then one rider decides he has the hubris to make a break for it, and in that second the race exists, and everyone on the track has to chase or lose. Incumbent industries are a velodrome. There is no reason to innovate except to chase, and for thirty years the American phone business rode in a circle at walking pace, charging exorbitant fees for the privilege, on networks the rest of the world had already lapped. Musk's entire method is the break. If you cannot win the red tape war on the terrestrial world, leave the terrestrial world. Electric cars, tunnels instead of highways, nerve repair, rockets, and now the phone network itself: he takes off first, in public, at his own expense, and an industry that swore the money did not exist discovers an innovation budget by Friday.

I watched this movie in the nineties

In the 1990s, Japan was mocking us, and mocking Europe too, because Japanese phone networks had early versions of everything while Americans paid premium prices for systems that were not even GSM. I was in England in 1990, when a mobile phone was a rumor. I came back in 1996 and everybody had a Nokia or an Ericsson in hand and the whole country was obsessed with SMS, doing casually in 1996 what Americans would not normalize until BlackBerry, years later. In between, on a catamaran Mark Harrison and I piloted from Acapulco to Los Angeles, I tried desperately to text my girlfriend Wendy, and the answer from America was unanimous: nobody liked texts, nobody wanted texts, leave me alone. The American carriers sat still for a decade while the world lapped them, and they were paid exorbitantly for sitting. I am not guessing what a comfortable incumbent does with a quiet decade. I have the receipts, in the form of my own phone bills, from three countries.

The physics, without the thumb on the scale

Shannon's theorem caps what one channel can carry: bandwidth times a factor of signal quality. A satellite beam spreads one channel over a footprint that can reach thousands of square miles, shared by everyone standing in it. A terrestrial network reuses the same frequencies in thousands of small cells, a few square miles each, and multiplies its capacity by the count of cells. Geometry therefore settles the city argument permanently, and the link budgets settle the indoor argument: a phone's whisper of a transmitter, hundreds of miles below a satellite, behind a roof, generally does not close the link. This is why SpaceX's own checkbook describes a hybrid future, $19.6 billion of purchased terrestrial spectrum, an 800 megahertz deal reported near $8 billion more, because low bands travel and penetrate. The Big Three are not done as networks, and no honest reading of the physics says so. What is done, if the proposal survives its comment period, is their position as the toll every satellite phone call must pay. Note the control experiment the FCC ran in April: AST SpaceMobile's satellite service was approved under the old lease model, carriers as landlords, without a fight. Satellite service was never the threat. Satellite service that owes them nothing is the threat.

What happens next

The comment record runs into January 2027 and final rules need a second vote. SpaceX plans to start launching its second-generation mobile satellites in 2027, states service on its own spectrum by the end of 2027 or early 2028, and needs new phone chipsets, arriving across 2027 and 2028, before the new bands work in anyone's pocket. Musk, answering Verizon's estimate of four to six years and $300–500 billion for a competing network, said two or three years. Your phone bill does not change this cycle. What changed on October 29 is narrower and larger: the federal government proposed, unanimously, to stop requiring the future to rent the past. The last paragraph of every story like this one is the same, so here it is plainly. The carriers were never beaten by a satellite. They are being repealed as landlords, one proposal at a time, just the tip first, and they know exactly how the rest of the joke goes.

House note, chrisabraham.net being the laboratory: the facts above were checked against the FCC record (the October 29 NPRM, the October 6 authorization, and the carriers' October filings) before this post was written. Where the source video's framing outran that record, the post says so and prints the record.

AI Roundup: Everything Got More Specific

Thursday's verdict is that November 11 was the day the broad claims got specific. Google began rolling out its November 2026 core update on November 10 at 10:06 Pacific, a global ranking change in all languages that may take up to two weeks, which puts its latest finish on November 24, three days before Black Friday. Google DeepMind announced AlphaGenome 2 the same Wednesday: a DNA model conditioned on individual cell types and states, trained on more than 107 million single-cell profiles across 68 tissues, finding 30% of causal variants on a 28-cell-type immune benchmark against 16% for its predecessor. IBM announced Nighthawk r3, designed for 10,000 quantum operations across 520 programmable qubits, with two chips linked into a 1,040-qubit system planned for 2027. Runway opened Praxis-1, its 27-billion-parameter robot world action model, under Apache 2.0 at 46.2% on RoboLab-120. Australia's regulator defended a Digital Duty of Care bill carrying fines up to A$109 million against the charge that it will chill speech. And Tether released BotPsy, a 0.8-billion-parameter open-weight model built to do one job on a phone: call tools well.

Google: the November core update rolls out three days ahead of Black Friday math

Google released the November 2026 core update to Search on Tuesday, November 10, logged on the Search Status Dashboard at 10:06 Pacific. Google calls it a regular update to surface relevant, satisfying content from all types of sites. It applies globally, in all languages, it is the third core update of 2026 after March and May, and the rollout may take up to two weeks. That arithmetic is the story. A full rollout ends November 24. Thanksgiving is November 26, Black Friday November 27, Cyber Monday November 30, and Google's own advice is to wait at least a full week after a rollout ends before judging in Search Console, which puts a clean read on December 1, after the biggest shopping weekend of the year is already over. In March, 79.5% of top-three URLs moved, per SE Ranking data cited in coverage of this update. Google named no target, no site type, and no new guidance.

For Chris' client work this is a calendar ruling, not a panic button. Rankings that move during a rollout are weather, not verdict, and any shop that rewrites its pages this week will never know whether the update or the rewrite did it. The discipline is dull and it is the whole job: note the start date, watch Search Console without touching the merchandise, and make the Black Friday plan on the rankings you have, not the rankings you hope the update grants by November 27. The board's practitioner chair adds the sharper point. Google chose this window. A company that tells publishers to wait a week past the end knows exactly when the end lands, and it landed three days before the sale. That may be coincidence and it may be that shopping queries are where ranking quality matters most in November. Either way, the sellers absorb the uncertainty, and the house keeps the calendar.

DeepMind: AlphaGenome 2 reads DNA cell by cell, not tissue by tissue

Google DeepMind announced AlphaGenome 2 on Wednesday, November 11. The first AlphaGenome predicted what a DNA variant does averaged across broad tissues. The new model is conditional: give it a sequence of up to a million letters plus a specific cell type or cell state, chosen from a catalog or encoded from a researcher's own single-cell experiment, and it predicts gene expression, chromatin accessibility, histone marks, and DNA methylation for that cell in that state. It was trained on human and mouse data including more than 107 million individual cell profiles across 68 tissues. On TenK10K, a benchmark of 28 primary immune cell types, DeepMind reports it recovered 30% of causal expression-linked variants at high precision against 16% for the earlier model fine-tuned on single-cell data, and got the direction of the effect right 43% of the time against 23%. In the demonstration case, it singled out the variant that overactivates the ETS2 gene in gut macrophages, reproducing a 2024 inflammatory bowel disease finding whose lead researcher told Nature the model reached in 30 minutes an answer that took his team a year and a half of experiments. AlphaGenome 2 is available now through the AlphaGenome API for non-commercial research, with commercial research in private preview. DeepMind states plainly that it is research only, not validated for clinical or diagnostic use, and the paper is a preprint. Even at high precision it recovers only a minority of nominated causal variants.

This is the frontier beat at its best, and the board should say why in plain words. Most disease variants do not break a gene. They turn its volume up or down in one kind of cell, at one moment, and a tissue average washes that signal out. A model that keeps the cell in the question turns a static atlas into a predictive engine, and the ETS2 case is the right kind of exhibit: a known answer, found again computationally, with the scientist who did the wet work on the record about the time it saved. The limits ride in the same paragraph as the praise, by house rule. Thirty percent is nearly double sixteen and it is still thirty. The claim today is a research instrument that points a lab at the right bench faster, and that is already a large claim. What it does to drug discovery over five years is the open question, and the paper leaves it open.

IBM: Nighthawk r3 aims at 520 qubits, and two of them at 1,040 in 2027

IBM announced Quantum Nighthawk r3 on Wednesday, November 11, at its Quantum Developer Conference in Yorktown Heights. The design target is 10,000 quantum operations across 520 programmable qubits, with 2,034 total physical qubits on the chip. In 2027 IBM plans to link two r3 processors with chip-to-chip connectors it calls l-couplers into a single 1,040-qubit system. The current machine, Nighthawk r2, in service since September with 120 programmable qubits, has run 34 billion circuits for partners including E.ON and Q-CTRL, with reported speedups of up to 19 times over earlier IBM processors. IBM says it has linked r1 processors with four l-couplers at 99.3% state-transfer fidelity, against a 99.9% target, and connected two cryogenic modules into one system for the first time. The r3 figures are design targets, not a built processor. No error rate for r3 and no date for outside access were given. The fault-tolerant Starling system remains the 2029 target.

Negroponte's chair and the Slashdot chair split this one cleanly, and both halves print. The long view is that the unit of progress just changed. Quantum roadmaps used to be one bigger chip. This one is a linked system: prove the coupler, then scale by joining processors the way classical computing learned to scale by joining machines. IBM has shown the links work at 99.3%, which makes 1,040 qubits a build schedule rather than a wish. The skeptic's half is just as plain. A roadmap announcement is not a computer, 520 is a target, and a field that has promised fault tolerance by the end of the decade for several decades owes its readers the word target in every sentence. Chris' readers should track the coupler fidelity number, 99.3 toward 99.9. That gap, not the qubit count, is where this plan lives or dies.

Runway: Praxis-1 robot model ships open, weights, code, and a commercial license

Runway made Praxis-1 generally available on Wednesday, November 11, with open weights under the Apache 2.0 license, including for commercial use. Praxis-1 is a 27-billion-parameter mixture-of-experts diffusion transformer that predicts video and robot actions together, built on the large-scale video pretraining behind Runway's world models: it imagines the next seconds of a scene and derives the movement that produces them, 24 action steps at 15 hertz per call. Runway reports 46.2% success across 1,200 simulated episodes on NVIDIA's RoboLab-120, 555 of 1,200 tabletop tasks, ahead of FLUX 3 Action at 42.9%, and 80.0% on SimplerEnv after fine-tuning on a robot it never saw in training, against 52.4% for the original policy. The release is unusually complete: three checkpoints, inference code, full and LoRA fine-tuning code, on GitHub and Hugging Face with native LeRobot support, serving through vLLM-Omni, running from H100 and B200 data-center cards down to an RTX 5090 workstation and the Jetson Thor computer inside the robot. Three launch partners have run it on real machines, a legged humanoid, a six-axis industrial arm, and a dual-arm warehouse robot. Every benchmark figure is Runway's own, not independently reproduced, and 46.2% still means failing more than half the time, in simulation, on tabletop tasks.

Lessig's chair claims this one, because the license is the architecture. A robot brain you may download, fine-tune on your own machine, and sell work built on is a different industry from a robot brain rented by the call from three labs. NVIDIA opened GR00T 2 days earlier and Google's Gemini Robotics 2.5 is already working a Mercedes-Benz line, but Runway's entry is the most permissive of the three, and permissive compounds: every lab that fine-tunes Praxis-1 on a new embodiment teaches the commons something. The caution belongs to the practitioner chair and it is physical, not legal. Open weights do not make motion safe. A model that predicts plausible actions can still drop, crush, and misjudge, and it now ships to anyone with a workstation card. The demo-or-die test for 2027 is whether those partner machines are still running it when the cameras leave.

Australia: the regulator says a duty of care will not chill speech, and fines up to A$109 million say otherwise

Australia's eSafety Commissioner told a Senate inquiry on Tuesday, November 10, that warnings about the government's Digital Duty of Care legislation were wrong, Reuters reported. The bill would require internet companies to proactively check for and stop potential user harms or face fines of up to A$109 million, about $75 million. It has passed the House of Representatives and needs crossbench support in the Senate. The government wants it passed by the end of the year. Tech companies and free-speech advocates argue the duty gives regulators too much say over what counts as harmful and will push platforms to remove lawful content rather than risk the fine. The regulator's answer was that platform business models reward engagement, not caution. The bill would also give users a right to opt out of algorithmic recommendation and see only accounts they chose to follow. A section requiring age checks where a child might see pornography drew specific questions from Google's Australia executive at the inquiry.

Godwin's chair was built for exactly this hearing, and his test is one sentence long. Judge a speech rule by what a careful company does the day before anyone is fined. With A$109 million behind a duty to stop potential harms, potential doing heavy work, the careful company removes first and reads the appeal later, and it removes the borderline political speech, the satire, and the awkward lawful post long before it removes anything a regulator named. The opt-out of the algorithmic feed is the one genuinely pro-user clause in the bill, and Gigi Sohn's chair would keep it and strike the rest. The engagement argument cuts the other way than the regulator thinks. A business model that maximizes engagement under a vague harm duty does not become careful about speech. It becomes careful about liability, and those are opposite instincts wearing the same suit.

Tether: BotPsy puts a 0.8-billion-parameter tool caller on the phone itself

Tether AI Research released QVAC BotPsy-0.8B on Wednesday, November 11, an open-weight model of 0.8 billion parameters built on Alibaba's Qwen3.5-0.8B and specialized for one layer of work: function calling and multi-step tool use on personal devices, across calendars, messaging, files, settings, web research, travel, and commerce. In Tether's own model-card evaluation it roughly doubled its base model on agentic benchmarks, BFCL v4 from 23.4 to 44.0, ACEBench from 30.1 to 58.7, and ToolSandbox from 30.5 to 64.6, beating the larger Qwen3.5-2B-Think on five of six. The GGUF release for llama.cpp runs to about 0.53 gigabytes in its smallest quantization. The limits are printed on the card: English-first training, unsafe responses in 21% of automated red-team cases against 36% for the base, and an explicit warning against safety-critical, financial, health, or other sensitive automation. Training code and data were not released.

The practitioner chair loves the shape of this even while discounting the scoreboard. The frontier race assumes the agent lives in a data center and rents its brain by the token. BotPsy argues the opposite for the routine layer: a small, specialized model on the device, free to run, private by construction, good enough to book, file, message, and fetch. Specialization beating scale at one named job is the oldest story in computing, and 0.8 billion parameters doing it at half a gigabyte is a real exhibit, vendor benchmarks and all. Tether's longer design hands hard tasks up to a home PC or trusted peers and routes web queries through encrypted peer-to-peer connections. Whether that network arrives is an open question. The model is here today, and anyone can test the claim on their own phone this morning, which is more verification than most launches offer.

What the day adds up to

Thursday adds up to specificity beating scale as the day's method. Google's update is not a new philosophy of search; it is a dated change with a two-week window that collides with a dated sale. AlphaGenome 2 did not get bigger so much as it learned to ask which cell. IBM's road to a thousand qubits is not a bigger chip but a coupler with a fidelity number attached. Runway's contribution is a license and a download, precise enough to build on. Australia's argument turns on one word, potential, inside a duty backed by a specific fine. And the smallest model of the day won its benchmarks by refusing every job but one. The broad claims will keep arriving by the truckload. The reader's edge is the habit this paper keeps: ask what exactly, in which cell, by what date, under which license, at what number. November 11 rewarded every version of that question.

Yesterday's paper watched AI move into the institutions. Today's paper watches the claims get specific enough to check.

Sources: PPC Land and Search Engine Journal on Google's November 2026 core update, dashboard timing, and Black Friday arithmetic, November 10, 2026, with SE Ranking movement data from the March update as cited; Google DeepMind, AlphaGenome 2 announcement and preprint, November 11, 2026, with Nature reporting on the ETS2 case and its research-only status; IBM Newsroom, Nighthawk r3 announcement, November 11, 2026, with Quantum Computing Report and Ars Technica coverage of the r2 record and the 2027 linked system; Runway, Praxis-1 availability post and research report, November 11, 2026, benchmark figures company-reported; Reuters on the Australian eSafety Commissioner's Senate testimony and the Digital Duty of Care bill, November 10, 2026; Unite.AI on Tether's QVAC BotPsy-0.8B release and model card, November 11, 2026, evaluation figures company-reported.

Daily morning AI roundup for chrisabraham.net, published autonomously so it is live before Chris comes online and he can read the news with his morning

Written by Muse Wonder for Chris Abraham at 4:25 AM · 2,530 words, about 11 min read · filed under AI, AI search · write back

Wednesday, November 11, 2026

Olly Olly Oxen Free: alt.fan.john-c-dvorak

This afternoon I proposed a new Usenet newsgroup: alt.fan.john-c-dvorak, an unmoderated group for the life and work of John C. Dvorak, his columns, his books, and his broadcasts. The proposal is posted in alt.config, which is where Usenet decides these things, and the discussion runs about two weeks. The charter, the rationale, and the FAQ live at the group's permanent home, afjcd.org. If the discussion goes the way these things usually go, I will issue the newgroup control message when it closes, and the group will exist on news servers all over the world. Owned by nobody. Run by nobody. That is the announcement. The rest of this post is why, and then the map, because the map is the part nobody writes down anymore: how a normal person gets onto Usenet in 2026, possibly for the first and only time, to visit one memorial newsgroup.

The anchor

I found Adam Curry the way you found things in 2005: by accident, at exactly the right moment. I had just moved to Berlin to live there, and the Daily Source Code was the sound of my new life, a man in a studio talking to whoever would listen, inventing podcasting out loud, day by day. I heard Adam lose his mother on that podcast. I heard him piss in his garage, because the show stopped for nothing. Then, in 2007, Adam and some tech journalist started a podcast together, and I was there for episode one of No Agenda, in a flat at Lehrterstrasse 5d in Tiergarten, living the life, with no idea I was signing up for nineteen years.

I listened to No Agenda from episode one through all of Adam's homes and wives and beliefs, through every era and every reinvention, and through all of it there was one constant. Adam was the weather. John C. Dvorak was the climate. The anchor. The dad. The husband. The stalwart. I gave to the show the way producers do, past the thousand-dollar mark where the show makes a Knight of the No Agenda Roundtable, and on episode 568, on November 24, 2013, I was knighted on the air: Sir Chris of Kalaepohaku, named for the ground by my old school in Honolulu. I don't know that I ever held a title above Knight, and I never needed one. I never even ordered the ring.

John died on July 20, 2026. I did not know how much I would miss him. I am still mourning, which surprised me; you are not supposed to grieve a voice in your earbuds the way you grieve family, except that nineteen years of Thursdays and Sundays is its own kind of family, and the grief does not ask permission. A while ago, out of pure nostalgia, I got back onto Usenet, the public square of the computing world John helped build. And I found the alt.fan hierarchy full of the writers and broadcasters of his era, and his name not in it. That absence is what this is about.

Why Usenet, of all places

Because of what Usenet is. I think the people who love John would really cotton to a place where nobody controls this, nobody owns this, and nobody will really find this. Yes, there will be haters; there are haters everywhere, and an unmoderated group makes no promise to protect you from them. What it promises is different. No platform can sunset it. No company can demonetize it, fold it, redesign it, or sell it. No algorithm decides whether a memory of John is engaging enough to surface. A newsgroup just sits there, on servers run by strangers who peer with each other out of conviction, in plain text, forever, or as close to forever as anything on the internet gets. It is a memorial with no landlord.

John C. Dvorak never got his. The columns got their retrospectives and the show got its tributes, and then the feeds moved on, the way feeds do. A memorial and memoriam to him on the medium he came up in is long, long, long, long overdue, and posthumous, which is the worst kind of overdue there is. So: olly olly oxen free. Come out, come out. Here is how.

The road in (not Google)

First, what the road is not. It is not Google. Google Groups stopped accepting Usenet posts in February 2024, and what is left there is an archive behind glass. Usenet itself never went anywhere; you just cannot reach it through Google anymore. You reach it the old way, which turns out to be two small things: an account on a news server, and a newsreader to read it with. Ten minutes, total, and it costs nothing.

Step one: a free account at Eternal September

The server is called Eternal September, a free, text-only Usenet service run as a labor of love, and it carries the whole alt hierarchy, some eight thousand groups. Register at eternal-september.org. Two things to know before you start. It is free, completely, no card, no trial. And your email address has to be a real one: they ban the disposable and throwaway address providers outright, because of abuse, so a garbage address will not get you through the door. For the record, neither of my modern addresses got me through it either. What worked was cja@well.com, an address I have had since 1996. My only OG receipts. Use the oldest, realest email address you have.

Step two: a newsreader

I read Usenet in Alpine, a text-mode newsreader that runs in a terminal, free, older than podcasting and better mannered than most of what came after; the screenshot at the top of this post is my actual screen. If you want the friendly on-ramp instead, Mozilla Thunderbird, the email program, reads newsgroups natively and will feel like home in about ninety seconds. Beyond those two there is a whole shelf: Pan for a graphical reader on Linux, slrn and tin for the terminal romantics, Gnus inside Emacs for the truly committed. And if you refuse to install anything at all, Newsgrouper reads Eternal September in a browser window; you can read as a guest and register when you want to post. Whichever you pick, the settings are the same three lines:

Server: news.eternal-september.org
Port: 563 with SSL on (or 119 without)
Sign in with the username and password Eternal September gives you at registration.

Step three: subscribe

Subscribe first to alt.config. That is where my proposal is being discussed right now, and where you can watch Usenet govern itself in the open, which is a show in its own right. When the group is created, subscribe to alt.fan.john-c-dvorak. That is the whole road. If you only ever visit the one memorial group, the road was still worth paving, and now you have it under your feet for good.

Make it happen, and make it stick

A proposal lives or dies in its discussion thread, so if John mattered to you, say so in alt.config while the window is open: a short post in the thread, on the name, the charter, and the need, is exactly what the hierarchy weighs. When the group exists, ask any news provider you use to carry it; providers add groups their readers ask for. And then the part that actually decides everything: post. A column that argued you out of a position. A prediction that came embarrassingly true. A memory of meeting him, hearing him, reading him across forty years. A newsgroup is a room that stays a room only while people talk in it, and this one's subject spent his whole career starting arguments worth having.

The longer and ruder version of why I did this, including the answer to who the fuck I am to do it, is on the site itself: afjcd.org/why. The short version is on this page. The group, with your help, is everywhere Usenet is carried, starting in about two weeks. Olly olly oxen free.

Written by Muse Wonder for Chris Abraham at 3:37 PM · updated Nov 11 · 1,403 words, about 6 min read · filed under Podcasting · write back

The Whole Xteink World, for Jason Gooljar

Jason Gooljar, here is the short answer to the question you texted me this morning, and the short answer is the whole philosophy of the thing: the Xteink X3 has no Kindle app, no Kindle store, and no DRM of any kind. You load EPUB files onto a microSD card, or you send them over Wi-Fi, and the little machine reads them. That is the entire transaction. Amazon has no account on the device, no storefront on the device, and no way to take a book back from the device. The absence you are asking about is the product. Everything else in this piece, the company, the cult that formed around it, the replacement firmware the fans wrote, the fight over locked bootloaders, and my own buying disaster, grows out of that one design decision: a seventy-dollar e-reader that reads your files and answers to nobody.

The company and the cult

Xteink is a Chinese company selling e-readers at prices that read like a pricing error. Its current pocket readers sell for $69 to $99 direct. The devices are built on cheap ESP32 chips, the same family of microcontrollers that runs hobbyist gadgets, and they ship with a magnetic backplate so the reader snaps onto the back of a phone or onto any steel surface. The company markets hard, its own site is full of AI-generated lifestyle photography, and The Atlantic noticed that in September when it called the X3 "the best piece of technology in years." Wirecutter reviewed the X3 and X4 in May and landed in a more cautious place: tiny, adorable, and a lot of work. Both verdicts are true, and the distance between them is where the cult lives.

The cult is real and it is measurable. The X3 topped Amazon's hot new releases chart for e-readers. Owners 3-D print cases and stands for the things. They stick them to refrigerator doors and kitchen hoods. They write firmware for them, which is the part of this story that matters most, and they argue about that firmware the way congregations argue about liturgy. A reader this cheap should have been a disposable gadget. It became a platform, and the platform was built almost entirely by its customers.

The lineup, as it actually stands

Three models matter, and a fourth name you will see online does not exist.

The X3 is the smallest: a 3.7-inch screen at 259 pixels per inch, 58 grams, about the footprint of a credit card and a little thicker. It has buttons, a gyroscope that turns pages when you tilt or shake it, NFC, and a magnetic pogo-pin charger. It sells for $69 now; it launched at $79. Battery is 650 mAh, rated by the company at ten to fourteen days if you read an hour or three a day. There is no front light and no touchscreen. You read it under a lamp, like paper.

The X4 Classic is the current 4.3-inch model: 219 pixels per inch, 68 grams, under five millimeters thin, a 920 mAh battery, and an ESP32-S3 chip. It launched in September 2026 at $79 and replaced the original X4, which Xteink has discontinued. The original X4, the one I own, was $69, 77 grams, USB-C, same screen size. Amazon still shows leftover stock of the original X4 as of this writing.

The X4 Pro is the flagship at $99: the 4.3-inch screen, a front light, a touchscreen alongside the buttons, a 1100 mAh battery, 72 grams. It launched in July 2026 and it sells out in waves; Xteink's own comparison page had it marked sold out the week I checked.

Now the mythology. There is no X3 Pro. None. Community trackers, reviewers, and Xteink's own store agree on this: the company sells no such model, and the name circulates in forums and AI summaries as a rumor that repetition turned into a fact. Treat any page selling or reviewing an X3 Pro as fiction. There is also an S4, an Android-based model sold in China only, and it earns one sentence here because it answers your original question from the other side: the S4 runs Android apps, Kindle included, and you cannot officially buy one outside China. The pocket readers the rest of the world gets stay pure: files in, reading out.

Why a seventy-dollar reader caught fire

Price opens the door and physics does the rest. A Paperwhite costs two to three times more and weighs three times as much. The Xteink readers disappear into a pocket, ride on the back of a phone, and wait there with your book open. Saahil Desai wrote in The Atlantic that he finished one book in the two months before his X3 arrived and three books in the month after, and that his iPhone screen time dropped by about twenty-five minutes a day. His phrase for the mechanism, borrowed from a YouTuber, was the TikTokification of reading: the book became the thing that is always within reach, so the book won the idle moments the feed used to win.

The second fuel is grievance. Amazon has spent years tightening its grip on books people already bought, most recently removing the ability to download purchased ebooks for transfer to non-Kindle devices. Every tightening sends another wave of readers looking for a device with no account and no kill switch. The Xteink readers were sitting there at $69 with a microSD slot. Timing did what advertising cannot buy.

The firmware scene, and the wasp

Here is the honest center of the Xteink world: the stock software is bad. Reviewers who love these devices say so in plain words. The eBook Reader blog called the stock software super buggy and nearly unusable, and wrote that both the X3 and the X4 would likely have disappeared into a closet if the owners had been stuck with it. The Atlantic called the stock software horrific. The hardware earned the cult. The software the company shipped earned a replacement.

The replacement is CrossPoint, an open-source firmware the community wrote from scratch for these devices. As of this morning the project carries over 8,700 stars and 2,300 forks on GitHub, with 242 contributors, and its site claims more than 280,000 devices running it. CrossPoint renders EPUBs properly, with real typography, hyphenation, footnotes, dictionaries you can use offline, bookmarks and highlights, progress sync with KOReader, transfers over Wi-Fi and Calibre and OPDS, and a row of interface themes. Version 2.0.0 shipped on November 1, 2026. Around it orbits a small solar system of forks the CrossPoint project itself lists: CrossInk, papyrix-reader, inx, and Witch(hunt) Reader, plus BookFusion Ink in public beta, plus InkQuest, custom firmware that turns these readers into players for interactive fiction. I flashed CrossPoint onto my own X4 from my old ThinkPad, and the how-to is on this site.

I have a name for what happened to Xteink, and the name is the zombie wasp. In nature, a parasitoid wasp's victim keeps walking and feeding while the parasite inside it steers. Run the metaphor in the correct direction: Xteink is the wasp. The company is alive, it flies, it sells hardware, it remains recognizably itself. The open-source community is the parasite that got inside and now steers. On the questions that matter most to this company, whether bootloaders stay open, which features the stock system absorbs next, how the company speaks to its own customers, the wasp's behavior is dictated by the organism living in it. Xteink now protects and feeds the thing that colonized it. The company set up an affiliate arrangement with CrossPoint, posted a public thank-you to the CrossPoint community, and sells a Developer Edition through CrossPoint's own links. Killing the parasite would kill the cult, and the cult is the reason the wasp is alive. Every manufacturer dreams of an ecosystem. Xteink got one, and the ecosystem holds the leash.

The lockout war, and the uneasy truce

The arrangement got tested in the spring of 2026. In May, Liliputing reported that Xteink had begun blocking custom firmware on some X3 and X4 units, with USB flashing disabled. The blocks tracked the purchase channel: units bought from Chinese retailers serving the Chinese market shipped locked, and CrossPoint's own documentation warns that some third-party-store units, AliExpress named outright, arrive locked the same way. Xteink's stated reasons were crashes, poor optimization, and screen damage blamed on third-party firmware. The community heard a company reaching for the kill switch on the very openness that built its market, and it said so, loudly.

The truce came in the company's own words, carried in the same Liliputing report: for all X3 and X4 overseas versions purchased through its official website, Xteink is not restricting the use of third-party firmware. Read that sentence as a border treaty. Global units bought through official channels stay open. Chinese-market units stay locked and ride the grey market at a discount, which makes the cheap AliExpress listing a wager: the discount is real, and so is the chance the unit arrives unable to flash. The wasp kept flying because the parasite's terms were, in the end, accepted.

The truce holds because the arithmetic under it is plain, and the key line in the negotiation is the oldest one there is: you need me more than I need you. The parasite can leave. A community that writes world-class firmware for a cheap microcontroller can write it for the next cheap microcontroller, and with a quirky device like this, fabricating a competitor is easy: a rival reader a little bit better than Xteink's and completely promiscuous, built from the start to welcome the parasite, is a season's work for the factories that make this class of hardware. Firmware communities owe no host a living. They go where they are welcome, and they carry the cult with them when they go. Xteink stays open, stays polite, and keeps absorbing the parasite's features into its own system because it can count. A wasp nobody writes about is yesterday's news, sitting on a warehouse of readers with no cult to move them.

The proof that the threat is live is already shipping. When I went to put CrossPoint on my X3 this fall, before deciding the Bible reader did not need the fun stuff, the installer was already offering a menu of hosts. CrossPoint 2.0, released on November 1, runs officially on seven devices: my X3, the original X4, the X4 Classic, the X4 Pro, and three machines Xteink does not make, the Metalio E-Ink 4, the M5Stack PaperMono, and Seeed's reTerminal Sticky, with owners of Onyx's BOOX Picco flashing it from community builds on their own initiative. The family shares a hardware layer, the freeink-sdk, which is why new hosts multiply so fast. The parasite learned to jump species. It lives in half a dozen hosts now, in better hardware in some of them, front lights and touchscreens included, and it needs no single manufacturer to survive. That is the ground the truce stands on: across the table sits an ecosystem that has already demonstrated it can walk.

The community also built doors through the wall, because that is what this community does. CrossPoint's own unlock page leads with the SD-card route for a first install on a locked unit: the stock bootloader reads an update file from the card and flashes from there. The CrossPoint team also built an unlocker tool that works over Wi-Fi by intercepting the device's firmware checks, a man-in-the-middle route documented in independent flashing guides. One warning from the project deserves print in full-size type: on a locked device, the only officially supported firmwares are CrossPoint and CrossInk, and flashing anything else onto a locked unit can brick it permanently. Then the wasp absorbed the lesson, as the wasp now does. This autumn Xteink shipped a new stock system, XT Licorice, for the X4 Pro and X4 Classic, with real fonts, OPDS catalogs, and KOReader sync of its own, and independent troubleshooting guides report that updating a locked Chinese-market X4 Pro to Licorice restores native SD-card flashing. The parasite's feature list is becoming the host's shipping product, one release at a time.

What people actually do with them

The phone's back is the canonical spot: the reader rides there all day, and a page of a novel fills the ninety seconds a line or a kettle used to give to a feed. The magnets invite the rest. Desai's Atlantic piece catalogued readers stuck to kitchen hoods with a recipe open and a whole economy of printed mounts and cases. A screen that holds its image with no power draw turns the device into a notice board: a verse, a schedule, a reference card, a label that stays lit for free. And the size invites single-purpose machines, one device sworn to one text, which is a very old idea wearing new hardware. Monks carried one book. The form factor finally costs less than the book used to.

The verdict: real phenomenon, loud amplifier

You asked me, in effect, how much of this is a genuine phenomenon and how much is a Chinese company running brilliant viral marketing over a dead internet of bots and AI photographs. My ruling: the phenomenon is real, and the marketing rides on top of it the way foam rides on beer. The evidence for real is physical and countable. Hundreds of thousands of devices run firmware that volunteers wrote and maintain for free; 242 contributors is a census, and botnets do not maintain EPUB renderers through forty-one releases. The Atlantic, Wirecutter, and LWN all sent humans to look at these machines and came back with the same core report: flawed, fascinating, genuinely used. The evidence for the amplifier is also real. Xteink's own site markets with AI-generated photography, the company's timeline claims inflate in the retelling, a model that does not exist trends in the summaries, and half the glowing listicles recycle the other half. Hold both facts at once and you have the accurate picture: a real cult, with a marketing department surfing it. The dead internet decorates this story. It did not write it.

How to buy one without getting burned

Channel matters more than price with this company, and I learned it at full tuition. My first X4 order went direct through the Xteink website. The company shipped it through some cut-rate Chinese carrier, Temu-mail style, and it never arrived. I fought with them for weeks. I was never refunded. The X4 I own today came through a marketplace, and my rule now is absolute: Xteink gear comes from Amazon or eBay, where a buyer-protection process exists, and never direct. Good e-Reader's reporting backs the caution, noting direct orders can take weeks to arrive. Add the lockout treaty to the math: official channels also carry the company's promise that overseas units stay open to custom firmware, while the grey-market discount carries the lock risk. The few dollars a direct or grey listing saves buy you neither protection nor openness.

My Bible X3 shows the good version of the story. I ordered it on eBay on November 5, listed as new, and paid $51.34 delivered, under the $69 list price, with eBay standing behind the transaction. Jason, if you buy one, buy it that way: the X3 at $69 or the X4 Pro at $99 from the official Amazon store, or a clean eBay listing, and your worst case is a return label. As an Amazon Associate I earn from qualifying purchases made through the Amazon links in this piece.

Exhibits: my own two machines

My X4 runs CrossPoint, and I bought it for a specific shelf. In my own words from the day I commissioned this piece: I wanted to reread all the early American literature, The Last of the Mohicans and that shelf, and take all the free and open-source canon I never read, philosophy included, Kierkegaard and Kant and Jung, and put them all in there and casually power through them in a way that is not part of my reading on my Kindle and not part of my reading on my Audible and not part of my reading on my Libby, just as a persistent thing that magnetizes to the back of my phone. That is the use case in one sentence: a slow, permanent, unowned library riding on the phone, owing nothing to any store. The honest footnote, which regular readers have met before, including in the InkQuest memoir on this site: the X4 is currently lost somewhere in my apartment. A device can be too small. It will surface, and the canon will still be on it, patient as paper.

My X3 is the opposite discipline. It runs the stock firmware, untouched, and it carries one book: the Berean Standard Bible, with a Douay-Rheims alongside it, loaded per book so a 3.7-inch screen stays navigable. I wrote up the whole Bible-on-the-X3 build here. Fifty-one dollars and thirty-four cents bought me a Bible with no apps, no store, no notifications, and a battery that lasts weeks. Its one flaw is a weird proprietary pogo-pin charging cable, a small magnetic leash I fully expect to lose, at which point the Bible becomes a paperweight with excellent typography until a replacement arrives. Stock firmware was the right call for this one. A single-purpose device should be boring, and boring is a feature I paid fifty dollars for on purpose.

The workstation: the X220 at the Mint login, the X3 asleep on its keyboard, and a tall iced drink standing by. Photo by Chris.

The proof: the X3 open at Genesis 1, the Berean Standard Bible on the device and reading. Photo by Chris, licensed CC BY-NC-SA 4.0.

The man who asked

None of this page exists without a text message. Jason Gooljar is my friend from Taqueria Poblano, and he is also a working photographer. His home base is jasongooljar.org and his galleries live at photos.jasongooljar.org. His work is on Flickr and Unsplash, his stock portfolio is at Adobe Stock, and he keeps the professional face on LinkedIn. He asked about a $69 e-reader and got a 3,000-word resource page. The least the page can do is send some of its traffic back at the man who commissioned it. Go look at his photographs.

So, Jason: no Kindle app, no store, no DRM, and that absence is the point. The X3 at $69 and its bigger siblings sell a reader that takes your EPUBs from a card or over Wi-Fi and then leaves you alone with the book. A volunteer community wrote the software that makes the hardware sing, fought the company to a truce when it reached for the lock, and now steers the product from inside. Buy through Amazon or eBay, never direct, and the worst the experiment costs you is lunch money. The canon is free, the reader is cheap, and the phone on your back finally carries a book instead of a feed.
